- **[UI]** **The skeletons track smoothly, and the mannequin drawn over them is gone.** Four faults, and no single one of them could have been fixed alone. **The apparent motion blur was a literal blur:** each joint was a Gaussian-blurred disc at `.blur(radius: 4)`, and a soft-edged dot appearing somewhere new ten times a second reads as a smear. It is a stroked ring now, because a defined edge is what makes movement legible. **Nothing could interpolate:** `PoseJoint`, `PoseConnection` and `DetectedPose` all declared `let id = UUID()` and are rebuilt every frame, the same defect already fixed for the bounding boxes, so SwiftUI destroyed and recreated every joint on every sample. Identity is now what the thing actually is: a joint is its anatomical name, a bone is the pair of joints it spans, a skeleton is matched across frames by bounding box through `PoseIdentityTracker`, with humans and animals tracked separately so a person standing over a dog cannot inherit its limbs. **Bones could not animate even with identity**, because each was a `Canvas`, which draws imperatively and cannot be interpolated by SwiftUI whatever animation wraps it; they are `Shape`s with `animatableData` over both endpoints, so a limb sweeps rather than snapping, and that also removes the sixteen full-size `Canvas` allocations a single human skeleton used to stack over the preview. **The interpolation is linear at exactly 0.1s**, matched to the analysis interval: Vision samples at 10 FPS against a 120 Hz display, so nine frames in ten carry no new data, and a spring would be actively wrong here because evenly spaced samples would make it overshoot and wobble, adding motion the body never made. **Separately, the human silhouette is deleted.** It drew a mannequin from hardcoded fractions of the bounding box, head ellipse at 35% of its width, shoulders at 60%, waist at 40% of its height, legs as rectangles, so it drew the same figure every time stretched to whatever rectangle arrived and could not match a pose because it never read one. What made it read as wrong rather than stylised is that the real pose was already drawn beside it from `VNDetectHumanBodyPoseRequest`: two overlays described the same body and disagreed. The box says where the person is, the wireframe says what they are doing. The face overlay's eyes, nose and mouth go for the same reason, placed at fixed fractions with **no `DetectFaceLandmarksRequest` run anywhere in the app**; its oval stays, because the face rectangle is measured and inscribing an ellipse in it claims nothing the rectangle does not. Also: a bone's glow was stroked at full opacity regardless of confidence, so a 20%-confident limb kept a solid halo while its core faded. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: CameraOverlayGeometry.swift PoseIdentityTracker; CameraVisionOverlayView.swift BoneShape/WireframeJoint; 153 lines of invented anatomy removed]`

- **[UI]** **Two overlay faults that looked like layout and were not.** Detection chips printed on top of each other, "Appliance" over "Machine", "1 person" over "2 skeletons". The first repair assumed an `HStack` too narrow for three long words and moved it to `FlowLayout`, which fixed a real defect but not this one. The actual cause: the chip arrays were assigned inside `withAnimation`, and they render through `ForEach(id: \.self)` over strings, so a changed array is an insert plus a remove rather than a move. Animating that crossfades the outgoing set against the incoming one, and at ten updates a second both are on screen together. There is nothing to interpolate between two different words, so text now assigns outside the animation and replaces cleanly while geometry still animates. Second, region labels were clamped on the top edge only and carried a flat `x: 4` offset, which is fine until a box begins off-screen left; saliency produces exactly that, since a whole television is one salient object whose rectangle starts past the edge, and its label arrived sliced in half. Clamped in both directions now. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: device screenshots 2026-09-12 showing chip crossfade and clipped labels; CameraVisionOverlayView.swift handleFrameAnalysis and RegionBoundingBox offset]`

- **[UI]** **Onboarding follows the system appearance, which it was the only screen not to.** It painted its own three-stop navy gradient and drew about sixty-nine white and black literals on top. Nothing forced dark mode: `preferredColorScheme` appears once in the whole repository and it is in a telemetry view. So in light appearance the app around it turned white while the first screen anyone sees stayed navy. **The measure of how far outside the app it sat:** `DSColors` is used **511** times across the codebase and **zero** times in `OnboardingChecklistView`, and `@Environment(\.colorScheme)` was read nowhere at all. **One part was genuinely broken rather than merely inconsistent.** `INFOPLIST_KEY_UIStatusBarStyle` is `UIStatusBarStyleDefault`, which is *adaptive*, so in light appearance iOS draws dark status-bar glyphs; over a `Color(red: 0.03, green: 0.05, blue: 0.12)` backdrop that is near-black on near-black and the clock and battery disappear. It needed no override: once the backdrop follows the appearance the adaptive status bar is correct in both directions. **The repair is deliberately not a `DSColors` substitution.** Onboarding expresses hierarchy as an opacity ramp — solid for a headline, 0.75 subtitle, 0.6 explainer, 0.4 label, 0.2 timestamp, 0.08 hairline, 0.03 panel fill — and `DSColors` offers three text tokens and two separators, so mapping onto it would collapse seven levels into three and trade the design away for the adaptivity. `OnboardingPalette` resolves one `ink` colour per appearance and every site became `palette.ink(0.6)`, keeping the ramp and changing only what it is a ramp of. The light backdrop is not the dark one inverted, which would be a flat pale wash: it is warm off-white falling to a faint blue-grey, the scrim turns white because black would dirty the screen, and the glows drop to 45% since they read as light sources on navy and as stains on off-white. **The primary buttons were the actively wrong part**, black text on a white fill, which in light mode is a white button on a near-white backdrop; they now invert against the backdrop. Two strays fixed alongside: the launcher pill's `Color.white.opacity(0.15)` hairline, invisible on light, and `definitionUnderline`'s **default parameter**, which was white and therefore a trap for any future caller that did not pass one, onboarding having always passed explicitly. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: OnboardingPalette.swift; OnboardingChecklistView.swift, now zero colour literals; DSColors 511 uses app-wide against 0 in onboarding; project.pbxproj:779 UIStatusBarStyleDefault]`

- **[UI]** **The camera uses every lens the phone has, which is what makes close-up focus work.** The session opened `AVCaptureDevice.default(.builtInWideAngleCamera, ...)`: one physical camera, one minimum focus distance, and nothing to do about a subject closer than that except go soft. It now runs a `DiscoverySession` preferring `builtInTripleCamera`, then `builtInDualWideCamera`, then `builtInDualCamera`, then the single wide, so a Pro gets ultra-wide, wide and telephoto presented as one device and a non-Pro still works. **Auto-macro is not a setting and cannot be requested**; it is what a virtual device does on its own. `AVCaptureDevice.h` describes the mechanism: when a scene requires focus beyond the limits of the active camera, the device switches to one with a shorter focal length and a smaller minimum focus distance, the *fallback primary constituent device*. With a single physical camera selected there was nothing to fall back to, which is the whole reason close-ups never sharpened. `autoFocusRangeRestriction` is deliberately left untouched, because restricting it to `.far` is how macro gets disabled by accident, and focus is set to `.continuousAutoFocus` with smooth autofocus enabled to damp the pulsing that otherwise shows in a live preview. **One trap handled:** on a virtual device `videoZoomFactor` 1.0 is the *widest* constituent, so a triple camera opens on the ultra-wide and the preview would have launched at what reads as 0.5x with distorted edges. The starting zoom is taken from `virtualDeviceSwitchOverVideoZoomFactors.first`, the factor at which the main wide takes over, so it opens at the familiar 1x computed from the device rather than hardcoded, since those factors differ between phones. Also fixed alongside: `setFlash` was doing its own `.builtInWideAngleCamera` lookup and would have locked and configured a different `AVCaptureDevice` than the one capturing. A log line names the chosen device, its constituents and its minimum focus distance. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: AVCaptureDevice.h fallback-primary-constituent-device description and virtualDeviceSwitchOverVideoZoomFactors, iPhoneOS27.0.sdk; CameraManager.bestAvailableRearCamera/configureFocusAndZoom]`

- **[Orchestration]** **Adaptive profiles reach Deep Think and Maximum, and you can write your own reasoning profile.** Two gaps in the feature as first shipped, both closed. **The profile applied to Standard only**, because its gate sat below the `if useAgentic` return and the agentic modes leave before it; that was the honest fix at the time, since the alternative was a log line claiming a change that never reached generation, but it left the feature absent from the two modes where a per-intent temperature matters most. It is now resolved in `executeAgenticQuery` and held on the service for the query, the same pattern `activeUserRoutingPreference` already uses, which exists because the model picker turned out to be inert in these exact modes for this exact reason. `generateWithProperConsent` takes `temperature: Float?` rather than a hardcoded `0.5`, so precedence runs explicit step value, then profile, then the old default. **Exactly one of its twelve call sites passed a temperature**, so the comment reading "higher for exploration, lower for synthesis" described an intent the code had stopped expressing; making the parameter optional is what surfaced that. `maxTokens` is deliberately not taken from the profile on the agentic path, where per-step budgets exist for what each step is doing and one intent-derived ceiling would truncate mid-chain work unrelated to final answer length. **Second, `ContextOptions.ReasoningLevel` has a fourth case nobody had used:** `custom(String)`, free text describing how to approach a problem rather than a dial between light, moderate and deep. A field in Model Parameters writes one and it replaces the named level. Two properties are pinned by test because both fail silently otherwise: a custom profile **does not start reasoning where the level was `.none`**, since that case means the query does not warrant the spend and "think very hard" must not turn every cheap lookup into a billed PCC reasoning request; and it **does not leak onto the on-device route**, where Apple lists reasoning as unsupported and there is no level to replace, which is what keeps the settings copy about on-device answers true. 6 tests. `[evidence_level: test_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: FoundationModelRoute.swift reasoningLevel; RAGService.activeAdaptiveProfile; AgenticOrchestrator generateWithProperConsent; CustomReasoningProfileTests 6 cases; ReasoningLevel.custom read from iPhoneOS27.0.sdk FoundationModels.swiftinterface]`

- **[UI]** **The camera names arbitrary objects and boxes them, which Vision has no request for.** Pointing it at a kitchen previously produced the chips "Structure, Wood Processed, Furniture" and not one box, because the only Vision requests that return a **labelled** box are people, faces, cat/dog, barcodes and text. `ClassifyImageRequest` knows roughly a thousand categories and has no spatial extent at all: it describes the whole frame. There is no request that finds arbitrary objects and names them, and a general detector is assembled rather than called. `GenerateObjectnessBasedSaliencyImageRequest` returns `salientObjects`, real rectangles around whatever stands out, carrying no labels; each one is cropped out of the frame and classified on its own, so the label describes that region instead of the scene. **Written on the Swift Vision API** (`GenerateObjectnessBasedSaliencyImageRequest`, `ClassifyImageRequest`, `RectangleObservation`) rather than the `VN*` classes the rest of `CameraManager` uses: those are not deprecated, but they are the Objective-C-era surface from iOS 11 and the Swift one arrived for iOS 18. Migrating the other eight requests is tracked separately rather than smuggled in alongside a feature. **The region carries the classification confidence, not the saliency score**, because "how sure are we this is a refrigerator" is what a reader of the label cares about. **Object regions are exempt from the overlay's confidence slider**, which judges every detector against one number while their confidences are not comparable: `.fast` text reports a flat 0.5, face rectangles report high values, and image classification reports genuinely low ones, so a correct "Refrigerator" near 0.3 would have been hidden at the 0.5 default and read as the detector finding nothing. They are gated where they are produced, at 0.25. Capped at four per frame because each costs a classification, and cached by quantised position so a stationary object is named once rather than ten times a second. **Stated limit:** saliency finds what stands out, not an inventory. A cluttered counter yields the few things that draw the eye and a flat evenly-lit wall yields nothing; calling this "everything in frame" would be the same overclaim as the YOLO service that searched for a model file this app has never contained. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: full Vision request enumeration from iPhoneOS27.0.sdk Vision.swiftinterface, 25 requests, none producing labelled general-object boxes; SaliencyImageObservation.salientObjects; CameraManager.labelledSalientObjects]`

Now "Look at Something" with a viewfinder icon. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: AttachmentPicker.swift]`

- **[UI]** **The camera overlay stopped burying the thing it was pointed at.** Device screenshots after the geometry fixes showed the boxes landing correctly and the view still unreadable, for reasons that were entirely presentation. **Every box carried a label capsule** at a fixed `-24pt` offset with no collision handling, so a page of printed text produced one capsule per recognised line, stacked on each other, covering the document being read; text regions now get no capsule at all, because the box already says where the line is and the Live Text panel already says what it says. **Every capsule read "50%"**: `VNRecognizeTextRequest` in `.fast` mode reports 0.5 for essentially every observation, so the confidence number was the same meaningless value repeated across the screen, and it now renders only for the types whose confidence actually varies. **The diagnostics HUD defaulted to on** and covered roughly 40% of the viewfinder, three metric pills, eight per-type counters and a slider inside a material card re-blurring live video every frame; it is off by default and one tap away on the chart button, being a developer's view of the screen rather than a user's. **The scene chips were a plain `HStack`** that could not fit three long classifications on a phone, so SwiftUI compressed them into each other and rendered "Computer" on top of "Consumer Electronics"; they use the `FlowLayout` the object chips already used. Labels near the top edge now sit below their box instead of behind the notch. **And the frame rate:** body-pose estimation is the most expensive pair in the batch and returns two empty arrays when no body is in frame, which is exactly the document case the owner measured at **3 FPS**; both pose requests now run only when the previous frame contained a human or an animal, which cannot latch off because the cheap rectangle requests that set that flag still run every frame. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: four device screenshots 2026-09-11 showing the capsule pile-up, the repeated 50%, the HUD occlusion and the chip overlap; CameraVisionOverlayView.swift, CameraManager.swift request gating]`

- **[UI]** **The live camera overlay was wrong in four independent ways, and the first one made every box meaningless.** Enabling the screen exposed it; the owner's verdict on first sight was that it looked like shit, which it did. **1. Nothing ever told AVFoundation or Vision which way is up.** No `videoRotationAngle` on any connection and no `CGImagePropertyOrientation` on any request, anywhere in the repository. Buffers arrive in sensor orientation, so a portrait-held phone fed Vision an image rotated a quarter turn: normalized X ran down the screen and Y ran across it, every box was transposed with width and height swapped, and text recognition, which tolerates skew but not 90 degrees, mostly returned nothing. `AVCaptureDevice.RotationCoordinator` now drives the connection's angle and re-applies it via KVO as the device turns, so the buffers themselves arrive upright and every consumer benefits rather than each request needing a hint. **2. Every box was a new view every frame.** `DetectedRegion` declared `let id = UUID()` and the regions are rebuilt per frame, so `ForEach` saw a wholly new identity set each update and SwiftUI destroyed and recreated every box. The two `interpolatingSpring` animations written against those views could never run, because a spring interpolates a surviving view and none survived; `smoothDocumentRegions` was defeated the same way. The boxes were not jittering, they were strobing. `RegionIdentityTracker` now carries identity across frames by intersection-over-union within a type. **3. The coordinate transform ignored the preview's crop.** Five identical copies of `minX * containerSize.width` stretched Vision's normalized space onto the view's bounds, while the preview layer is `.resizeAspectFill`, which scales the image to cover and crops the overflow. On a 1920x1080 buffer shown portrait roughly 18% of the image width is off-screen, so boxes were compressed by about 0.82 and pulled toward the centre, worsening toward the edges. Because aspect-fill crops symmetrically the centre stayed correct, which is why it read as intermittently wrong rather than always wrong. All five now call one tested function. **4. The throttle was in dead code.** `analyzeFrame`, which carries the 10 FPS guard, barcode detection and a text-height floor, has **zero call sites**; the live path is `performFrameAnalysis`, which ran eight Vision requests synchronously on the serial analysis queue for every delivered frame, each followed by a GPU drain and a sleep, while AVFoundation silently dropped the backlog with no `didDrop` implementation to notice. The guard now sits on the path that runs. Also removed an empty `Task { @MainActor in await MainActor.run { } }` that cost a task allocation and two main-actor hops per frame to do nothing. 15 tests over the geometry and the tracker, including one that asserts the new transform **disagrees** with the old naive one away from the frame centre, so deleting the aspect compensation fails a test rather than silently restoring the defect. `[evidence_level: test_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: CameraOverlayGeometry.swift, CameraOverlayGeometryTests.swift 15 cases; AVCaptureDevice.h RotationCoordinator and AVCaptureSession.h isVideoRotationAngleSupported in iPhoneOS27.0.sdk; zero repo-wide grep hits for CGImagePropertyOrientation and videoRotationAngle before this change]`

- **[UI]** **Every camera entry in this release is experimental, by the owner's own description (2026-09-13).** The screen was unreachable from v1 until this cycle, everything in it was built and repaired over two days, and its behaviour in a real room has been seen by one person on one phone. The arithmetic is tested and the API use is checked against the SDK; neither of those is evidence that it finds the right things. **Updated the same day:** the user-facing notes carried a "Your Camera (experimental)" section, which stopped being the right thing to ship the moment the screen was gated to Debug builds, because `Docs/USER_CHANGELOG.md` is copied verbatim into `OpenIntelligence/Resources/VersionHistory.md` and rendered in Settings, so every 5.3 user would have read about a "Look at Something" button their build does not have. That section is held in `Docs/Release/5.3/camera_user_notes_held.md` for whichever release carries the camera. Nothing here should be promoted to settled language, or cited as a shipped capability in Settings copy, the listing or the roadmap, until real use exists behind it.

- **[UI]** **The camera is reachable again, because there is finally a detector behind it worth reaching.** `CameraVisionOverlayView` has been in the tree since v1, complete: a preview layer, a bounding-box overlay with the Vision-to-SwiftUI coordinate flip, pose wireframes, live label chips and three-frame temporal smoothing. Roughly 4,300 lines under `Features/Camera/` compiled into every shipped iOS build and ran for nobody. It was off at **three** independent points, any one of which hid it: `ChatScreen` passed `onVisionCapture: nil`, `AttachmentPicker` guards its Scan Document button on `if let onVisionCapture` so the button never rendered, and the presenting `fullScreenCover` was commented out under `// v2 feature - disabled for v1 App Store release`. **Leaving it off was the right call until today**, because the detector behind it could not work: it searched for four YOLOv3 `.mlmodelc` files that have never existed here, always fell through to `VNClassifyImageRequest`, which returns labels with no spatial extent, and gave each one a full-frame rectangle so the overlay had something to draw. Switching the screen on before fixing that would have shipped five boxes drawn around the entire frame, stacked. **Correction, made the same day this entry was written:** the sentence that stood here claimed `LiveObjectDetectionService` was the detector behind this screen. It is not. `CameraVisionOverlayView` takes everything from `CameraManager.onFrameAnalyzed`, which runs its own `VN*` pipeline; `LiveObjectDetectionService` is wired to `DocumentCaptureView`, which is still unreachable. So enabling this screen shipped the old pipeline, and the owner's verdict on seeing it, that the live detection looked like shit, was correct and had four separate causes. See the camera-overlay entry below for what they were. The presenter is inside `#if os(iOS)` and the action is a computed `visionCaptureAction` rather than an inline conditional in an argument list; `NSCameraUsageDescription` was already set via INFOPLIST_KEY, so the permission prompt has copy. **Not device-verified:** a live camera feed cannot be exercised in a simulator, so this builds and links and has never been pointed at a room. **Superseded 2026-09-13, later the same cycle:** "reachable" now means reachable in a Debug build. The action is gated `#if os(iOS) && DEBUG`, so 5.3 ships with the screen unreachable; see the gate entry at the top of this section. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, not_device_verified, evidence_source: ChatScreen.swift visionCaptureAction and the restored fullScreenCover; AttachmentPicker.swift:349 guard; CameraVisionOverlayView.swift]`

- **[Orchestration]** **The app now records what Apple actually spent on a generation, not just what it asked for.** `LanguageModelSession.usage` is new in iOS/macOS 27 and was read nowhere. It carries `reasoningTokenCount` separately from output tokens, which is the only signal in the public SDK distinguishing a model that reasoned from one that answered directly: `Response` carries no route or backend field at all, so `actualRoute` was the sole claim about where an answer came from, and it is a claim about intent rather than an observation of outcome. A PCC route reporting zero reasoning tokens on a Deep Think query is now a visible contradiction. Read from the **session** rather than a `Response`, because the streaming path never sees a `Response` and a `Response.usage` approach would have covered the structured path and silently skipped streaming, which is the same omission this instrument exists to detect. Also captures `input.cachedTokenCount`, the first measurement of prompt caching in this app. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, evidence_source: LLMService.swift GenerationUsage/readUsage; Swift 6.4 build links 37 GenerationUsage and 20 Usage symbols against a non-zero PrivateCloudCompute control. NOT test-verified: xcodebuild test fails before reaching any test.]`

- **[Ingestion]** **Object detection stopped looking for a model this app has never contained.** `YOLODetectionService` was written against Apple's downloadable YOLOv3 CoreML model: `loadModel()` searched the bundle for `YOLOv3Tiny`, `YOLOv3`, `YOLOv3TinyInt8LUT` and `YOLOv3Int8LUT` as `.mlmodelc`, **none of which exist in this repository or ever did**. So `isModelLoaded` was permanently false, the 80-entry COCO class list it carried was read by nothing, and every call fell through to a fallback of `VNClassifyImageRequest` plus `VNRecognizeAnimalsRequest`. Image classification returns labels with **no spatial extent**, and the fallback gave each one `CGRect(x: 0, y: 0, width: 1, height: 1)` so it would satisfy the overlay's box-drawing code, meaning a feature described as "object detection with bounding boxes" could at absolute best box an animal, and would otherwise have drawn five rectangles around the whole frame stacked on each other. `LiveObjectDetectionService` replaces it with five detectors that ship in the OS and need no download: `DetectHumanRectanglesRequest` (people, with an upper-body flag), `DetectFaceRectanglesRequest`, `RecognizeAnimalsRequest`, `DetectBarcodesRequest` (decoded payload and symbology) and `ClassifyImageRequest`. The first four return real geometry. The fifth does not, and its results now carry `isSceneLevel` instead of a fabricated full-frame rectangle, so a caller can render them as text. Each detector runs through `VisionOCRThrottle` separately, so one failing does not take the rest of the frame's results with it, and a CoreML detector is still supported through `configure(withModelNamed:)` if a real model is ever added, merged with the built-ins rather than replacing them. **When this entry was written it did not make the camera reachable**, because `ChatScreen` still passed `onVisionCapture: nil` and the presenting `fullScreenCover` was still commented out. That changed later the same day; see the camera entry below. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, evidence_source: LiveObjectDetectionService.swift; request and observation shapes read from iPhoneOS27.0.sdk Vision.swiftinterface 2026-09-11, including NormalizedRect.cgRect and QuadrilateralProviding's inherited boundingBox]`

### Changed
- **[Orchestration]** **Generation settings can now follow what the question is asking for, instead of one temperature per quality mode.** `RAGQualityMode` carries a single temperature for every question asked in that mode, so Standard answered a serial-number lookup and an open-ended comparison identically at 0.4. The answer intent is a better signal and `QueryEnhancementService` has already resolved it before generation. `FoundationModelDynamicProfileRegistry` maps it to one of four profiles: extractive for lookups, table lookups **and calculations** (0.10 / 400 tokens, because a derived number has one right answer and sampling variance in arithmetic is a wrong total rather than creativity), grounded for procedures (0.30 / 900), analytical for comparison and investigation (0.35 / 1400), summarization (0.40 / 1000). **The registry existed before this and was a placeholder that would have made answers worse.** It mapped each intent to a complete replacement `systemPrompt`, one-line strings such as "You are a helpful assistant", which would have substituted a generic sentence for the grounding, citation and abstention rules `FoundationModelPromptCompiler` assembles. It had zero call sites and `SettingsView` had already pulled "Dynamic Profiles" from the capability list as a false claim. It now produces generation parameters only and touches no prompt. **Off by default and opt-in**, in Model Parameters, because the effect on answer quality is unmeasured and currently unmeasurable here: retrieval is nondeterministic, two runs of one build return different evidence, so no A/B in this repository is trustworthy yet, and shipping it on would change everyone's answers on a hypothesis. `UserDefaults.bool(forKey:)` is false for an absent key, so nothing changes for an existing install. Applying a profile is a pure function returning a modified copy and **writes nothing back to settings**, unlike `AutoTuneService`, which adjusts the same two values by persisting them over what the owner set by hand and survives turning the feature off; it has zero call sites and a test now pins that this one does not behave that way. **It applies to Standard only.** Deep Think and Maximum return earlier via `executeAgenticQuery`, which never reads this config for generation: it builds `optimizedConfig` from `qualityMode.agenticConfig` and hands that to `AgenticOrchestrator`. The first version of this change applied the profile above that branch, which mutated a local the agentic path never reads while logging `temperature 0.4 -> 0.35` as though it had taken effect. A log asserting a change that did not happen is worse than no feature, so the gate now sits below the branch and the limit is stated rather than papered over. Reaching the agentic path means changing where `AgenticOrchestrator` gets its generation parameters, tracked separately. 10 tests. `[evidence_level: test_verified, confidence: high, evidence_source: FoundationModelDynamicProfileRegistry.swift, RAGService.swift adaptive gate, AdaptiveInferenceProfileTests.swift]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Vision's data detection was being computed and thrown away, and five regexes were used in its place.** `RecognizeDocumentsRequest` runs data detection in the same pass that produces the transcript and hands the results back on `DocumentObservation.Container.Text.detectedData`. `StructuredDocumentParser.extractDetectedData` read none of it: it took the `Text` value, used only `.transcript`, and re-derived entities from hand-written patterns, under a comment saying full integration "requires additional API verification". That verification is done, against `DataDetection.swiftinterface` in the iOS 27 SDK. The patterns matched email addresses, **US-format** phone numbers, `http(s)` URLs, **three** currency symbols and two numeric date shapes, so a European invoice yielded no amounts and a postal address was invisible. Apple's detector returns ten categories including postal addresses split into street, city, state and postal code, calendar events as real `Date` values, money as a `Decimal` plus a `Locale.Currency`, measurements, flight numbers, shipment tracking numbers and payment identifiers. It also returns **parsed values rather than matched substrings**, so `DetectedEntity` now keeps both: `value` is the normalised form and `rawText` is the text as printed, because a reader may search for either. The regexes are kept rather than deleted and run only for the types Apple returned nothing for, because `detectedData` is empty whenever the parse degraded to plain text recognition, which is exactly the low-quality scan where one email address may be the only thing worth extracting. **A stated limit:** this function is called from `parseTable` only, so entities still come from table cells and not from paragraphs; closing that gap touches several element-construction paths and is tracked separately. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: high, evidence_source: StructuredDocumentParser.swift extractDetectedData/entity(from:in:)/patternEntities; Docs/INGESTION_PIPELINE.md §2.7]`

### Fixed
- **[UI]** **Three sliders in Model Parameters said they affected sampling, and they affect nothing this app can run.** Frequency Penalty, Presence Penalty and Repetition Penalty sat under the footer "These parameters affect token sampling behavior", which is false for both selectable models. `GenerationOptions` in the iOS 27 SDK exposes exactly `samplingMode`, `temperature`, `maximumResponseTokens` and `toolCallingMode`; the string "penalty" does not occur anywhere in the FoundationModels interface, so Apple Intelligence ignores all three on device and on Private Cloud Compute. The values are collected, persisted, threaded into `InferenceConfig` by `ChatScreen` and carried all the way to generation, where they are silently dropped because Apple has nowhere to put them. That is what made the screen read as confusing: three controls that move, save, and change nothing, under a sentence insisting they do. **The sliders are deliberately kept**, which a comment in that file has documented since before this change: their consumer, `LocalOpenAIServerLLMService`, is scaffolding for the roadmap row "Bring-your-own local model on Mac (third-party model host)", which is still open, and deleting the controls would delete a planned feature's UI. The same comment described this footer as the defect and as already fixed. It was not; the original sentence was still shipping, and this is the change that actually makes it true. `repetitionPenalty` is additionally marked in its own caption, because unlike the other two it is not read even by that scaffolding and would still do nothing on the day the local model host ships. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: iPhoneOS27.0.sdk FoundationModels.swiftinterface GenerationOptions; every reference to the three values across OpenIntelligence and OpenIntelligenceTests; roadmap row status read 2026-09-11]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **The test suite could not build, and the cause recorded for it was wrong.** `xcodebuild test` failed to link `OpenIntelligenceEngine.framework` with every `Tokenizers` symbol undefined. The explanation written into `Docs/ai/STATE.md` and the roadmap on 2026-09-10, that the Engine target declared no `packageProductDependencies`, was false and came from an inspection regex capped at 1200 characters that truncated the target block before reaching the declaration. The real cause is a level deeper: `OpenIntelligence/swift-transformers` is a **shim** whose entire source is `@_exported import Tokenizers`, and the module it re-exports comes from a separate remote package, `swift-tokenizers` 0.7.1. Four Engine sources import `Tokenizers` directly, so their symbols live in a framework Xcode builds and leaves unused in `PackageFrameworks/`, because Xcode links a product's transitive package dependencies into an **application** target and not into a **framework** target. That is why the app, the simulator smoke build and Xcode Cloud were all unaffected: the test bundle is the only thing that links the Engine as a standalone unit. Wiring only the Engine moved the failure rather than fixing it, because declaring the product explicitly anywhere turns off the automatic inference the app had been relying on, so both targets now declare it. `scripts/fix_engine_tokenizers_link.py` performs and documents the edit, and reads the target block with no character cap, deliberately. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: full Ld command lines before and after; Tokenizers_<hash>_PackageProduct.framework present in PackageFrameworks and absent from the Engine link line]`
